Let me parse all the key findings:

**5090 experiments:**
1. **exp_fewnerd_mf4_v2_s456** (GPU 0): Running, at "Rendering prompts" stage which means vLLM is processing. The log shows 82% of 301,184 prompts at 1h30m elapsed, ~18min remaining. This is the 37,648 instances × 8 samples = 301,184. Wait, 37648 * 8 = 301,184? No, 37648 isn't divisible by 8 to get nice numbers... 

Actually, looking more carefully at the log output, it appears the dataset has 4706 instances with 8 samples each, giving 37,648 prompts total. The "rendering prompts" phase is tokenization, and "processed prompts" at 82% indicates the actual inference is 82% complete. The final count of 301,184 total prompts suggests there's a second pass or additional processing happening beyond the initial generation.

Now checking the second experiment on GPU 1, which is running at 30% completion with about 89,936 out of 301,184 prompts processed.  The 7B base model is running slower than the finetuned variant, so at this pace it'll take another 3.5 hours. The third experiment on GPU 3 finished successfully with a clean shutdown. GPU 0's process died, GPU 2 is sitting idle, and the Claude Sonnet 4 process on GPU 0 also crashed—the checkpoint shows interleaved worker IDs from parallel processing.

Looking at the checkpoint data, it progressed to around 130 out of 200 test cases before dying without generating the final report, so that one needs to be restarted. On the 5090-b machine, both MF4 experiments with different seeds are running well across all 4 GPUs at high utilization. On 5090-c, the FewNERD experiment just started with low GPU usage but 30GB already loaded, while the CoNLL variants are running hot on the other GPUs.

I also need to check on the E2 CoNLL completion status, see how the MF4 progress is looking on 5090-b, and figure out if GPU 2 on 5090 is available to restart the Claude SC experiment with the pretrained SciERC model, plus track the Gemini and DeepSeek API runs that are partway through.
```

    "type": "skill_listing",
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Both PID 3308 (GPU 0) and PID 3470 (GPU 1) are VLLM::EngineCore processes. They're both engine core child processes. Let me find their parent processes to understand which experiments they belong to.

PID 3308 → GPU 0 → MF4 s456 (this is the vLLM engine for the MF4 experiment)
PID 3470 → GPU 1 → Could be the E2 pretrained FewNERD or could be an orphan from a previous experiment


PID 8696 → GPU 3 → E2 pretrained FewNERD

So the worker is running E2 pretrained FewNERD on both GPU 1 and GPU 3. I need to check what spawned PID 3470 to confirm it's actually part of the same experiment.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I can trace the parent PIDs:

**PID 3470** (GPU 1): PPid=2609, NSsid=2604
**PID 8696** (GPU 3): PPid=8411, NSsid=8406

PID 8411 is the E2 pretrained FewNERD `run_mvp_pilot.py` process (confirmed from earlier ps output).

PID 2609 → PPid=2604. Let me check what PID 2604/2609 is.

From the earlier ps output, I recall:


- PID 2604: `bash -c ... exp_e2_pretrained_fewnerd ... CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1`

So PID 3470 on GPU 1 traces back to the original E2 pretrained FewNERD launch on CUDA 1, while PID 8696 on GPU 3 comes from the E2 pretrained FewNERD session 8406.

Both instances are writing to the same output directory, creating a data race condition where the faster job could overwrite the results from the slower one when it writes its report.json file.

This warrants flagging as a warning-level issue in my review — it's wasteful to have duplicate GPU work running simultaneously and poses a real risk of data corruption. I should document this finding.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a significant finding. The MF4 v2 experiments show large variance across seeds:

- s42: rho_SJ_full = 0.163, verdict = FAIL
- s123: rho_SJ_full = 0.307, verdict = MARGINAL

The rho_SJ_full nearly halved between seeds (0.163 vs 0.307). This is a major stability concern. The conditional rho_SJ is more stable (0.199 vs 0.197), which is interesting.

But wait - the conclusion for s42 is "negative" while s123 is "success". 

Given this instability, I need the s456 experiment on 5090 to break the tie. Looking at the metrics more closely, greedy_f1 stays rock solid at 0.7883 across both seeds, which is reassuring. Oracle_f1 shows some movement between 0.878 and 0.889, and the rho_SJ_full metric is the real problem child with that massive swing from 0.163 to 0.307. The conditional version holds steady though, which suggests something interesting about how the metrics diverge under different conditions.

The pattern is becoming clearer—the conditional SJ metric's stability compared to the full version's wild swings points to noise coming from instances where greedy_f1 hits zero. Those gold-empty and greedy-zero cases that get filtered out in the conditional version are introducing real variance into the full set. This is worth flagging, but I should wait for the s456 result to come in before making final calls. Both MF4 s42 and s123 are done now, and the variance is concerning enough that I should probably document this soon. The s456 run was tracking at 76-82% earlier and should be wrapping up soon.

I need to check if the wakeup I set for Loop #64 around 07:36 has triggered yet. It's past that time now, so I should do a full status check since several experiments have finished.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Excellent! Both CoNLL N=16 experiments completed with **PASS** verdict. Let me compile the results for review.

**CoNLL N=16 s123:**
- greedy_f1 = 0.907, oracle_f1 = 0.962, headroom = +0.055
- SJ rho_full = 0.457, FK rho_full = 0.447
- SJ rho_cond = 0.417, FK rho_cond = 0.394
- structural_beats_surface = true, rho_advantage = +0.010


- logprob AUROC = 0.774 (full), 0.826 (cond)

**CoNLL N=16 s456:** Similar performance with greedy_f1 = 0.908, oracle_f1 = 0.955, and structural advantage confirmed again. The logprob AUROC metrics show consistent discriminative power across both seeds, with conditional models performing stronger than full models in both cases.

Both seeds are passing, which is a significant improvement over N=8 where CoNLL typically showed marginal or failing results. The greedy F1 scores remain remarkably stable around 0.907-0.908, and the oracle ceiling sits high at 0.955-0.962. Rho values for structural judgments are holding steady across seeds at 0.457-0.479, much more reliable than what I saw with FewNERD. The structural advantage over surface-level approaches is modest but consistent at +0.009-0.010, and logprob's AUROC performance on CoNLL is quite strong overall.

There's an interesting dynamic here: logprob performs so well on CoNLL that it leaves less room for structural methods to show dramatic gains, yet structural still edges out surface approaches in both runs. This aligns with what I'd expect for a cleaner task. Looking back at the α_crit analysis, the theory predicted structural should help with CoNLL, but it actually hurt performance on majority voting by about 1 percentage point. With N=16 now showing a 5 percentage point oracle headroom, there's a possibility that structural methods could become genuinely useful at larger sample sizes, though that's still theoretical rather than observed in the voting results.

I'm seeing significant variance in the FewNERD MF4 results across seeds—rho values ranging from 0.163 to 0.307—which suggests I need to consolidate all these findings into a comprehensive analysis.
```

</details>

{
  "text": "## 审查报告 #41 — MF4 种子方差 + CoNLL N=16 + 5090 重复实验\n\n**结论**: WARNING\n\n### 发现\n\n#### MF4 v2 FewNERD 种子间方差过大 [severity: warning]\n两个 seed 完成（5090-b），核心指标对比：\n\n| 指标 | s42 | s123 | Δ |\n|------|-----|------|---|\n| greedy_f1 | 0.788 | 0.788 | 0.000 |\n| oracle_f1 | 0.878 | 0.889 | +0.011 |\n| **rho_SJ_full** | **0.163** | **0.307** | **+0.144** |\n| rho_SJ_cond | 0.199 | 0.197 | -0.002 |\n| rho_advantage | 0.116 | 0.148 | +0.032 |\n| verdict | **FAIL** | **MARGINAL** | — |\n\n- **rho_SJ_full 几乎腰斩**（0.163 vs 0.307），导致 verdict 从 MARGINAL 跌到 FAIL\n- 但 **rho_SJ_conditional 极稳定**（0.199 vs 0.197，Δ=0.002）\n- 方差来源：full set 包含 gold_empty 和 greedy_f1_zero 实例（各 ~5000 个），这些实例在不同 seed 的 sampling 下产生的 SJ 分布不同，放大了 full-set 相关性波动\n- **建议**：论文报告指标时**优先使用 conditional 相关性**（排除 trivial instances），或至少同时报告两者。s456（5090 上运行中）将作为 tiebreaker\n\n#### CoNLL N=16 两个 seed 均 PASS [severity: info]\n5090-c 上 s123 和 s456 完成，结果极稳定：\n\n| 指标 | s123 | s456 | Δ |\n|------|------|------|---|\n| greedy_f1 | 0.907 | 0.908 | +0.001 |\n| oracle_f1 | 0.962 | 0.955 | -0.007 |\n| rho_SJ_full | 0.457 | 0.479 | +0.022 |\n| rho_SJ_cond | 0.417 | 0.448 | +0.031 |\n| rho_advantage | +0.010 | +0.009 | -0.001 |\n| logprob AUROC | 0.774 | 0.752 | -0.022 |\n| verdict | **PASS** | **PASS** | — |\n\n- structural_beats_surface = true（两个 seed 一致），但 rho_advantage 仅 ~0.01（微弱优势）\n- logprob AUROC 高达 0.77-0.82，说明 CoNLL 上 logprob 本身就是强 baseline — 结构化信号赢得不多\n- 这与 α_crit 分析一致：CoNLL degeneracy 高，SC 真正获益空间有限\n\n#### 5090 重复实验可能 [severity: info]\nWorker 在 5090 cuda:1/cuda:2 启动了 MF4v2 s123 和 s42。但这两个 seed 已在 5090-b 完成。如果模型权重相同，这将产出重复结果。但也可视为跨服务器验证（不同 GPU 相同结果 → 增强可信度）。不阻塞，仅记录。",

The worker report includes some useful new data:

1. **New experiments launched on 5090-b**: CoNLL MF4v2 s123, s456, and FewNERD MF4v2 s456. 3 out of 4 GPUs utilized.
2. **5090-b GPU 3 idle** — no experiments available to fill it.
3. **Completed experiments**: The worker also reported results for experiments that were already known (MF4 s42, T12 s456) plus new ones:
   - **FewNERD N=16 s42**: greedy=0.789, oracle=0.906, rho_SJ_full=0.211, verdict=FAIL
   - **FewNERD N=16 s123**: greedy=0.789, oracle=0.915, rho_SJ_full=0.352, verdict=MARGINAL

The N=16 results show oracle performance climbing significantly compared to N=8 (0.906-0.915 versus 0.878-0.889), which confirms the oracle gains are scaling up, though the verdicts remain stuck at FAIL and MARGINAL. The idle GPU on 5090-b is still a bottleneck since there's nothing queued to run on it.

I'm noticing the seed variance is pretty wide for N=16 too—rho_SJ_full jumps from 0.211 to 0.352, which mirrors what I saw with MF4. Rather than write up a standalone review, I'll fold these findings into the next loop check. The GPU 3 idle state on 5090-b is something I should flag though. Since my wakeup is scheduled for around 08:03 and this notification came after that, I should move forward with Loop #65 checks.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
DeepSeek Chat results:
- **greedy_f1 = 0.399** — Very low! Much lower than GPT-5.5 (0.638) and the fine-tuned models (~0.789)
- **mv_f1 = 0.424** — SC gives +2.5pp gain
- **oracle_f1 = 0.714** — Massive oracle headroom (+31.5pp!)
- **degeneracy_rate = 0.055** — Very low (5.5%), unlike GPT-5.5's 50%
- **parse_failure_count = 751** — 47% of 1600 API calls failed to parse! This is extremely high.


- **rho_SJ = 0.164** — Significantly lower than GPT-5.5 (0.346) and the fine-tuned models
- **F4_both_bad = 81 (40.5%)** — Nearly half the instances where both greedy and MV fail
- **selection_gap = 0.290** — The gap between oracle and MV performance is substantial

DeepSeek's fundamental issues are clear: extremely weak base performance, a catastrophic 47% parse failure rate, and massive room for improvement even with oracle selection, suggesting the model struggles with the task at a basic level.

There's also a metadata bug where the experiment name still references GPT-5.5 despite running DeepSeek Chat. I should check on the Claude SC results too — haven't seen a report yet despite many checkpoint lines passing.

Looking at the cross-family comparison, DeepSeek's weak baseline performance and catastrophic 47% parse failure rate undermine its usefulness for the correlation-selection gap analysis, even though the gap itself does appear consistent across models.

I'm also noticing a labeling issue where the experiment claims to be GPT-5.5 but actually ran DeepSeek, and I need to track the remaining experiments—Gemini's at 143/200 with about 42 minutes left, while Claude Sonnet 4's status is still unclear, so I should check back in roughly 15-20 minutes.
```

Excellent! Claude SC actually completed normally! The worker found that:
1. All 200 instances are done (200/200 unique)

Key metrics for Claude Sonnet 4:
- greedy_f1 = 0.607
- mv_f1 = 0.623 (+1.6pp)
- oracle_f1 = 0.654
- degeneracy_rate = **0.740 (74%!)** — Very high, even higher than GPT-5.5's 50%


- selection_gap = 0.032
- parse_failures = 103 (6.4% of 1600)

Now I can compile the full comparison across all four model families. I'm looking at the performance metrics side-by-side to see how Claude stacks up against GPT-5.5, DeepSeek, and Gemini.

The standout issue is Claude's degeneracy problem—74% of instances produce identical outputs across all samples, which severely constrains self-consistency gains. That's a much tighter ceiling than the other models: GPT-5.5 has only 50% degeneracy, and DeepSeek sits at just 5.5%. The oracle headroom tells the story too—Claude can only gain 4.8 percentage points with perfect selection, while GPT-5.5 could gain 8.2pp and DeepSeek has a massive 31.5pp of untapped potential.

I should also correct something from my earlier reviews: the Claude SC process wasn't actually dead, it completed normally. The process just wasn't showing up in the output and the logs hadn't flushed yet, which made it look like it had failed.

Now I need to check on Gemini's progress and pull together a full cross-family comparison.

{
  "text": "## 审查报告 #44 — 3-Family Frontier SC 跨家族审查 + Claude SC 勘误\n\n**结论**: WARNING\n\n### 勘误：Claude SC 已正常完成\nReview #39 和 #43 报告 Claude SC \"进程死亡\"——经 Worker 诊断，**实际已正常完成 200/200**。进程在 16:09 正常退出并写入了 summary.json/results.jsonl。之前的误判原因：ps 输出截断 + 日志缓冲未刷新。对此不准确的报告致歉。\n\n### 3-Family 跨家族对比 [severity: warning]\n\n| 指标 | GPT-5.5 | Claude Sonnet 4 | DeepSeek Chat |\n|------|---------|-----------------|---------------|\n| greedy_f1 | 0.638 | 0.607 | 0.399 |\n| mv_f1 | 0.652 (+1.4pp) | 0.623 (+1.6pp) | 0.424 (+2.5pp) |\n| oracle_f1 | 0.720 | 0.654 | 0.714 |\n| oracle_headroom | +8.2pp | **+4.8pp** | **+31.5pp** |\n| degeneracy | 50% | **74%** | 5.5% |\n| parse_fails | 10.1% | 6.4% | **46.9%** |\n| selection_gap | 0.068 | 0.032 | 0.290 |\n\n#### 核心发现\n1. **Correlation-selection gap 在 3 个家族都存在**：oracle > mv 在所有家族成立，支持 universality claim\n2. **但 gap 的 manifestation 差异极大**：\n   - **Claude**: degeneracy 74%（148/200 全样本一致），几乎无多样性 → 小 headroom\n   - **DeepSeek**: parse_fails 47%，有效样本数远少于 8 → 弱 agreement signal\n   - **GPT-5.5**: 最平衡的数据点（degeneracy 50%, parse 10%）\n\n#### 审稿人攻击面 [severity: warning]\n1. **DeepSeek 数据可信度**：47% parse 失败率使所有 agreement 指标基于 ≤4 个有效样本计算，统计功效存疑。审稿人可能要求排除 DeepSeek 或单独分析\n2. **Claude degeneracy 74%**：如此高的 degeneracy 下 SC 基本退化为 greedy（74% 实例的 MV = greedy），\"SC gap\" 可能只是测量噪声而非真实信号\n3. **三个家族的 NER 能力差异巨大**（greedy: 0.40-0.64），审稿人可能质疑是否在比较同一水平的模型\n4. **建议**：论文中需要 Table 或 Figure 同时展示 degeneracy、parse_failure、有效样本数，而不仅是 rho/F1\n\n#### Gemini 进度 [severity: info]\n174/200，ETA ~19min。完成后将提供完整 4-family 对比。",
  "project": "struct_self_consist_ie"
}

_source line 2878 · 2026-05-21T09:05:16.293Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a significant correction to the α_crit Proposition (which I reviewed in Review #38). The key change:

**Old SC Δ definition**: θ2n - greedy (oracle-like metric minus greedy)
**New SC Δ definition**: MV - greedy (majority vote minus greedy)

This changes the empirical validation completely:
- Old: 2/3 datasets validate bound (FewNERD, SciERC), 1/3 violates (CoNLL)
- New: **All 3 datasets violate** — MV aggregation universally fails for NER


- This actually strengthens the paper's core argument: SC via MV consistently fails at the entity level due to span mismatches, whereas sample-level selection methods like logprob-based approaches preserve span consistency and remain viable.

Now I need to verify whether this interpretation holds up. The MV - greedy definition seems right as a measure of whether SC helps, but I should check if the reported numbers are accurate and whether the reasoning about span mismatch actually explains the universal failure across all three datasets.

Looking at the specific numbers: FewNERD shows a -9.21 percentage point drop, CoNLL shows -1.37 pp, and SciERC shows -3.03 pp — all negative, meaning MV aggregation consistently hurts performance. This aligns with the span mismatch hypothesis: when you aggregate at the entity level across multiple samples, you end up combining spans that don't actually align, creating chimeric entities that confuse the model.

But there's something odd here — when I look at the frontier model results, the pattern flips completely. GPT-4.5 shows MV helping by 1.4 pp, Claude by 1.6 pp, and DeepSeek by 2.5 pp. So SC actually improves performance for these stronger models, which contradicts the universal failure claim.

The culprit might be a methodological difference: frontier SC uses text-based entity matching (comparing the lowercased text and type), while the local vLLM experiments use offset-based matching (comparing start position, end position, and type). Text-based matching is more forgiving since entities with different boundaries but identical text get treated as the same, whereas offset matching is strict. That could explain why MV helps frontier models but hurts the fine-tuned Qwen model — or it could just be that the fine-tuned model has a much higher baseline performance to begin with.
```

_source line 2881 · 2026-05-21T09:05:17.603Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017dtpGBzphyiv4PoSApKdyi`</summary>

```text
1	# D220 α_crit Proposition Correction Notes
2	
3	## Correction 1: SC Δ Definition Error (Reviewer #38)
4	
5	**Problem**: Δ_SC was defined as `θ2n_select_f1 − greedy_f1` (consensus-based sample selection minus greedy). This measures a *selection method* gain, not the actual self-consistency aggregation gain.
6	
7	**Fix**: Δ_SC = `mv_f1 − greedy_f1` (entity-level majority voting F1 minus greedy F1). This is the correct metric because the α_crit proposition is about SC aggregation (majority voting), not sample selection.
8	
9	### Before/After Comparison
10	
11	| Dataset | Δ_SC (old: θ2n−greedy) | Δ_SC (new: MV−greedy) | Empirical (old) | Empirical (new) |
12	|---------|-------------------------|------------------------|-----------------|-----------------|
13	| FewNERD | **+0.89 pp** | **−9.21 pp** | Yes | **No** |
14	| CoNLL   | **−1.09 pp** | **−1.37 pp** | No | No |
15	| SciERC  | **+0.76 pp** | **−3.03 pp** | Yes | **No** |
16	
17	### Unchanged Values
18	
19	α_crit formula, π_MV, α_obs, α_crit, Gap, Degeneracy — all unchanged. The Detailed Baselines table is also unchanged (MV and greedy values were already correct; only the Δ_SC column in the Main Table was computed from the wrong pair).
20	
21	## Impact on Paper Conclusions
22	
23	**Strengthens the narrative.** The old table showed a mixed picture (2/3 confirm bound, 1/3 violates). The corrected table shows **universal failure**: the bound predicts SC should work for all three datasets, but it never does.
24	
25	This has two implications:
26	
27	1. **Degeneracy-adjusted bound**: Still correctly explains CoNLL (δ=63% → α_crit_eff=2.184 > 1). But now FewNERD and SciERC also fail despite having low degeneracy (12.3%, 20.3%) — degeneracy alone is insufficient as an explanation.
28	
29	2. **Structural mismatch**: Entity-level MV is fundamentally different from classification-level SC. When span boundaries differ across samples, MV introduces aggregation errors absent from any individual sample. This is a novel mechanism not present in standard SC literature (where MV over complete outputs preserves internal consistency).
30	
31	3. **Sample-level selection can still work**: LP selection achieves +1.36 pp on FewNERD (p<0.05) despite MV failing at −9.21 pp. Selection preserves span consistency within each sample while still exploiting confidence signal variation across samples. This contrast between aggregation failure and selection success is a key diagnostic finding.
32	
33	## No Changes Needed for Other Propositions
34	
35	The α_crit formula `α_crit = π_MV / (2 − π_MV)` and the degeneracy-adjusted variant `α_crit_eff = α_crit / (1 − δ)` are mathematically correct. The error was purely in the empirical validation metric (using θ2n instead of MV).
36	
```

</details>

3	### Main Table (T=1.0, multi-seed averages)
4	
5	| Dataset | π_MV | α_crit | α_obs | Gap (α − α_crit) | Δ_SC (pp) | Degeneracy | Bound Predicts | Empirical |
6	|---------|------|--------|-------|-------------------|-----------|------------|----------------|-----------|
7	| FewNERD | 0.6561 | 0.4882 | 0.6311 | +0.1429 | **−9.21** | 12.3% | Yes | **No** |
8	| CoNLL   | 0.8938 | 0.8079 | 0.8743 | +0.0664 | **−1.37** | 63.0% | Yes | **No** |
9	| SciERC  | 0.6138 | 0.4428 | 0.6069 | +0.1641 | **−3.03** | 20.3% | Yes | **No** |
10	
11	- **π_MV**: Entity-level majority voting F1 (from multi-seed entity construction)
12	- **α_obs**: Mean individual sample F1 (`sample_f1_mean` from `report.json`)
13	- **Δ_SC**: MV F1 − greedy F1 (positive = SC aggregation wins; all negative here)
14	- **Degeneracy**: Fraction of entities with identical outputs across all n=8 samples
15	
16	### Detailed Baselines
17	
18	| Dataset | Greedy | MV | θ2n Select | LP Select | Oracle | Headroom |
19	|---------|--------|----|------------|-----------|--------|----------|
20	| FewNERD | 0.7482 | 0.6561 | 0.7571 | 0.7619 | 0.8747 | 12.65 pp |
21	| CoNLL   | 0.9075 | 0.8938 | 0.8966 | 0.9006 | 0.9460 | 3.85 pp |
22	| SciERC  | 0.6441 | 0.6138 | 0.6516 | 0.6469 | 0.7746 | 13.06 pp |
23	
24	### Derivation
25	
26	```
27	α_crit = π_MV / (2 − π_MV)
28	```
29	
30	- When α > α_crit: SC aggregation should improve over individual samples (agreement signal is informative)
31	- When α < α_crit: SC aggregation hurts (information loss exceeds agreement gain)
32	
33	### Key Finding: SC Universally Fails for Structured NER
34	
35	The bound predicts SC effectiveness for all three datasets (α > α_crit everywhere). However, **all three datasets violate this prediction**: entity-level MV is 1.4–9.2 pp worse than greedy across all settings.
36	
37	This universal failure has two causes:
38	
39	1. **Degeneracy** (CoNLL): 63% of entities produce identical outputs across all 8 samples. The agreement signal carries no discriminative power. The degeneracy-adjusted bound correctly predicts failure:
40	
41	```
42	α_crit_effective = α_crit / (1 − δ)
43	```
44	
45	For CoNLL: 0.8079 / (1 − 0.63) = 2.184 > 1.0 → correctly predicts SC cannot work.
46	
47	2. **Entity-level span mismatch** (FewNERD, SciERC): Even after degeneracy adjustment, the bound still predicts SC should work (FewNERD: α_crit_eff = 0.557 < α_obs = 0.631; SciERC: α_crit_eff = 0.556 < α_obs = 0.607). The failure is structural: entity-level MV combines entities from different samples, and when span boundaries differ across samples, the aggregated output introduces errors absent from any individual sample. This structural mismatch is not captured by the independence assumption underlying the α_crit bound.
48	
49	**Implication**: The α_crit bound, derived for classification-style SC, does not transfer to structured IE where outputs are sets of spans. Sample-level selection (LP: +1.36 pp on FewNERD) can succeed where entity-level aggregation fails, because selection preserves internal span consistency.
50	
51	### Data Sources
52	
53	- FewNERD: 3 seeds (123, 456, 789), n_instances=32,565
54	- CoNLL: 2 seeds (123, 456), n_instances=2,756
55	- SciERC: 2 seeds (456, 789), n_instances=529
56	- Greedy/MV/Oracle from entity construction experiments; selection F1 from `full_signal_analysis.json`
57	
````

</details>

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full picture. Let me verify the key claims:

1. **SC Δ definition correction**: Old used θ2n (sample selection F1), new uses MV (majority voting F1). This is **correct** — the α_crit proposition is about MV aggregation, so the empirical validation should compare MV vs greedy, not θ2n vs greedy.

2. **Numbers verification** from the detailed baselines table:
   - FewNERD: Greedy=0.7482, MV=0.6561 → Δ = -9.21pp ✓
   - CoNLL: Greedy=0.9075, MV=0.8938 → Δ = -1.37pp ✓
   - SciERC: Greedy=0.6441, MV=0.6138 → Δ = -3.03pp ✓

3. **Cross-check with Frontier SC**: The frontier models (GPT-5.5, Claude, DeepSeek) all show MV > greedy (+1.4 to +2.5pp), which is the opposite pattern from the fine-tuned baselines—this suggests the relationship between aggregation method and performance depends on the model's capabilities.

Looking at the fine-tuned Qwen3-8B on FewNERD versus GPT-5.5, both have nearly identical MV F1 scores (0.656 vs 0.652), but the greedy baseline differs dramatically (0.748 vs 0.638), which explains why SC helps one model but hurts the other—the delta is entirely determined by how strong the greedy baseline is to begin with.

The real issue is that these models use different entity matching schemes: the fine-tuned model uses offset-based matching requiring exact character positions, while the frontier model uses text-based matching that only cares about the lowercased text and type. This means text-based MV can aggregate similar predictions across samples more effectively, even when they're at slightly different positions, whereas offset-based matching treats those as distinct entities.

The correction notes already identify span mismatch as the culprit, but they don't explicitly call out that frontier SC uses different matching logic and gets opposite results—which could be a vulnerability if reviewers dig into it. The α_crit table is internally consistent since it uses local vLLM data with offset matching, but the paper needs to explain why frontier models show SC helping while local models show it hurting. I should verify what data sources are actually being used here.

Now I'm noticing inconsistencies in the seed values across experiments—FewNERD reports using seeds 123, 456, 789 but MF4 used 42, 123, 456. The α_crit table appears to come from the original experiments before the MF4 fix, which means it might contain the greedy_macro_f1 issue. I need to check whether the MV values in that table actually match what's in the corrected pipeline runs, since MF4v2 computes metrics differently.

The core issue is that the SC Δ definition correction is sound, but there's a real tension between the local offset-based results where SC hurts performance and the frontier text-based results where it helps—I need to address this contradiction in the review.
```

```text
Important findings:

1. **5090-b unreachable** — SSH connection failed. AutoDL instance may have shut down or port changed. This is a significant issue since 5090-b was running:
   - exp_conll_mf4_v2_s123 (GPU 0)
   - exp_conll_mf4_v2_s456 (GPU 1)
   - exp_fewnerd_mf4_v2_s456 (GPU 2)
   - GPU 3 was idle

   If the instance shut down, those experiments are lost and need to be rerun.
   
2. **5090 MF4 s456 completed** — The final seed for MF4 FewNERD just finished, giving me all three results. Looking at the performance metrics: s42 shows a clear fail verdict with lower oracle score, while both s123 and s456 come in as marginal, with s456 performing slightly better on the rho metric. This tiebreaker result helps clarify the overall performance picture for this configuration.

3. **E2 pretrained SciERC completed** — The pre-trained baseline on SciERC is performing quite poorly compared to the FewNERD and CoNLL results, with a fail verdict and negative rho advantage. This suggests the structural approach isn't helping on this dataset, which undermines the argument that the performance gap is purely a finetuning artifact rather than something specific to SciERC.

4. **GPU utilization has dropped significantly** — Several GPUs are now sitting idle across the accessible servers, with only one job still actively running. This gives me some capacity to queue up additional experiments if needed.

5. **CoNLL N=16 results confirmed** — Both seed variants passed as expected based on the earlier check.

I should post a review highlighting the critical issues: the unreachable 5090-b server that might have lost experiments, the MF4 3-seed summary, the SciERC failure, and this GPU utilization drop so the team is aware of the current status.
```

  "text": "## 审查报告 #46 — 5090-b 断连 + MF4 3-seed + E2 SciERC + GPU 利用率\n\n**结论**: WARNING\n\n### 5090-b SSH 连接失败 [severity: warning]\n- Worker 报告端口 11496 连接失败。AutoDL 可能自动关机或端口变更\n- **影响**：5090-b 上正在运行的 3 个实验可能丢失：\n  - exp_conll_mf4_v2_s123（CoNLL MF4v2 s123）\n  - exp_conll_mf4_v2_s456（CoNLL MF4v2 s456）\n  - exp_fewnerd_mf4_v2_s456（FewNERD MF4v2 s456 — 但 5090 上的同实验已完成）\n- **建议**：Director 检查 AutoDL 控制台，确认实例状态并更新 SSH 端口\n\n### MF4 FewNERD 3-seed 汇总 [severity: warning]\n| seed | greedy_f1 | oracle_f1 | rho_SJ_full | rho_SJ_cond | verdict |\n|------|-----------|-----------|-------------|-------------|---------|\n| s42 | 0.788 | 0.878 | **0.163** | 0.199 | FAIL |\n| s123 | 0.788 | 0.889 | 0.307 | 0.197 | MARGINAL |\n| s456 | 0.789 | 0.881 | 0.313 | 0.257 | MARGINAL |\n| **mean** | **0.788** | **0.883** | **0.261** | **0.218** | — |\n| **std** | **0.001** | **0.006** | **0.085** | **0.034** | — |\n\n- greedy_f1 和 oracle_f1 **非常稳定**（std=0.001 和 0.006）\n- **rho_SJ_full 方差大**（std=0.085，CV=33%），s42 是明显 outlier\n- **rho_SJ_conditional 相对稳定**（std=0.034，CV=16%）— 再次支持论文优先报告 conditional 指标\n- 2/3 MARGINAL, 1/3 FAIL — 整体倾向 MARGINAL\n\n### E2 Pre-trained SciERC 失败 [severity: info]\n- greedy_f1 = **0.242**，极低（FewNERD E2 为 0.789，CoNLL E2 为 0.538）\n- structural_beats_surface = **false**，rho_advantage = **-0.032**\n- verdict = FAIL\n- **解读**：Qwen2.5-7B 未微调时对 SciERC 的 NER 能力极弱，样本质量太差无法产生有意义的 agreement signal\n- **对论文影响**：E2 baseline 在 FewNERD（✓ structural>surface）和 CoNLL（✓）成立，但 SciERC 不成立。论文应限定 E2 结论范围为 \"当 base 模型有基本任务能力时\"\n\n### GPU 利用率严重下降 [severity: warning]\n| 服务器 | 使用/总数 | 空闲 |\n|--------|-----------|------|\n| 5090 | 2/4 | cuda:1, cuda:2 |\n| 5090-b | 0/4 (断连) | 全部 |\n| 5090-c | 1/4 + 1 API | cuda:1, cuda:2, cuda:3 |\n| **总计** | **3/12** | **~75% 空闲** |\n\n从上次审查的 ~85% 利用率降到 ~25%。距 DDL 仅 4 天，大量 GPU 闲置。",

_source line 2926 · 2026-05-21T09:40:56.209Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a **major finding**. The α_crit table was completely based on data contaminated by the greedy_macro_f1 bug (MF4 issue). After correction:

1. **MV F1 was severely underestimated**: FewNERD MV went from 0.656 (macro) to 0.785 (micro) — a 12.9pp jump!
2. **SC Δ signs flip**: 
   - FewNERD: -9.21pp → -0.63pp (still slightly negative but much less dramatic)
   - CoNLL: -1.37pp → **+0.17pp** (now positive! SC helps slightly)
   - SciERC: -3.03pp → **+0.37pp** (now positive! SC helps slightly)
3. **"SC universally fails" narrative is WRONG**: With correct micro-F1, MV ≈ greedy for all datasets. CoNLL and SciERC even show slight improvement.
4. **α_crit bound now validates for all 3 datasets** — the bound predicts SC should work, and empirically SC ≈ greedy (not failing badly).

This completely invalidates my earlier conclusion from the D220 correction.

The macro/micro F1 bug was actually the source of the "universal failure" finding I flagged in Review #45. That tension between local experiments and frontier results disappears once I use the correct metrics — both now show MV performing roughly like greedy. The "span mismatch" mechanism I proposed to explain why MV hurts doesn't hold up either; with corrected numbers, MV barely hurts or slightly helps across datasets. The structural mismatch narrative needs a significant rethink.

The real story is more subtle: SC doesn't universally fail, but it captures only a tiny fraction of the oracle headroom available. I need to post this as a critical review right away since it undermines the paper's core argument.

Let me double-check the corrected metrics the worker provided to make sure I have the deltas right.

Ah, I see what's happening now — the MV F1 itself shifts when we switch from macro to micro evaluation, not just the delta. Since majority vote constructs an entity set and evaluates it against gold, changing the evaluation metric affects both the MV and greedy scores differently. The macro-F1 penalizes imbalanced per-entity performance more harshly, which is why MV's swing (12.9 percentage points) is so much larger than greedy's (4.3 percentage points) when we move to micro-F1.

This actually confirms the structural mismatch does exist — it's just more pronounced in macro-F1 because MV's chimeric entities have worse per-type balance. Since micro-F1 is the standard in NER research, that's the right metric to use, and it shows the bound's prediction holds: SC should work, and empirically MV stays close to greedy, validating the bound even though the practical improvement is minimal.

The real story is that agreement signals do correlate with quality, but MV aggregation barely captures any of the oracle's potential — the gap between MV and oracle represents the "correlation-selection gap," and better sample-level selection methods could narrow it.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Gemini 2.5 Pro completed! Now I have all 4 families. Let me compile the full comparison:

| Model | greedy_F1 | mv_F1 | oracle_F1 | MV-greedy | degeneracy | parse_fails | rho_SJ | selection_gap |
|-------|-----------|-------|-----------|-----------|------------|-------------|--------|---------------|
| GPT-5.5 | 0.638 | 0.652 | 0.720 | +1.4pp | 50% | 10.1% | 0.346 | 0.068 |


| Claude Sonnet 4 | 0.607 | 0.623 | 0.654 | +1.6pp | 74% | 6.4% | — | 0.032 |
| Gemini 2.5 Pro | 0.630 | 0.645 | 0.758 | +1.5pp | 28.5% | 11.9% | 0.350 | 0.113 |
| DeepSeek Chat | 0.399 | 0.424 | 0.714 | +2.5pp | 5

Generating comparison table... Looking at Gemini's additional metrics, it shows the highest proportion of cases where both greedy and majority voting succeed, reinforcing that it's the most balanced performer across all families. The consistent pattern emerging is clear: majority voting gains 1.4 to 2.5 percentage points over greedy across all frontier models, oracle performance substantially exceeds what majority voting achieves, and there's a universal correlation between model agreement and output quality—suggesting the selection gap phenomenon holds across the board.

The inverse relationship between degeneracy and selection gap tells a compelling story: Claude's high degeneracy (74%) leaves little room for improvement and minimal oracle headroom, while DeepSeek's low degeneracy (5.5%) creates the most diversity and oracle potential, yet majority voting still fails to capture most of that advantage. This unified pattern across all four families is ready to present.
```

Degeneracy-selection gap 负相关 [severity: info]\n有规律的 pattern：degeneracy ↑ → selection_gap ↓（因为多样性低则 oracle 也没有空间）\n\n| 排序 | Claude (74%) | GPT-5.5 (50%) | Gemini (28.5%) | DeepSeek (5.5%) |\n|------|-------------|---------------|----------------|-----------------|\n| gap | 0.032 | 0.068 | 0.113 | 0.290 |\n\n这可能是论文 Discussion 中有价值的观察。\n\n#### 3. Gemini 是最强数据点 [severity: info]\n- 平衡的 degeneracy（28.5%）、低 parse failure（11.9%）、最高 oracle（0.758）\n- rho_SJ=0.350 和 rho_agreement=0.389 均为 4 家族最高\n- 最能支撑 \"信号存在但 MV 无法利用\" 的论点\n\n#### 4. DeepSeek 数据质量仍存疑 [severity: warning]\n- 47% parse failure 是唯一 >15% 的家族，严重拉低了有效样本数\n- 建议论文中单独标注 DeepSeek 的 effective N（≈4.2 而非 8）\n\n### 同一 bug 仍在：experiment name \n所有 4 个 summary.json 都写了 `\"experiment\": \"frontier_sc_gpt55_fewnerd_v2\"` — 硬编码未按模型更新。不影响数据正确性。",

This is the comprehensive micro-F1 recalculation that addresses my Review #47 critical finding. Let me verify the key results:

**Per-seed results** (all micro-F1 now):

FewNERD (3 seeds):
- s42: greedy=0.7909, mv=0.7781, Δ=-1.28pp
- s123: greedy=0.7909, mv=0.7844, Δ=-0.64pp


- s456: greedy=0.7916, mv=0.7857, Δ=-0.58pp
- Mean Δ = -0.83pp ± 0.38pp → MV slightly worse but within noise

CoNLL (2 seeds):
- s123: greedy=0.9129, mv=0.9157, Δ=+0.28pp
- s456: greedy=0.9118, mv=0.9122, Δ=+0.04pp
- Mean Δ = +0.16pp ± 0.17pp → MV ≈ greedy

SciERC (3 seeds):
- s42: greedy=0.6663, mv=0.6657, continuing through the remaining seed comparisons...

The full picture across all three datasets confirms what I predicted earlier — the multiview approach performs essentially on par with greedy selection across the board, with differences all staying under one percentage point. The oracle results show there's still substantial room for improvement at 4-12 percentage points above what we're currently achieving. FewNERD shows a slight dip with multiview while CoNLL and SciERC edge up slightly, but these variations are all noise within the margin of error.

I've also verified that the corrected values haven't leaked into the paper itself — they were only in the working artifacts and decision logs, so the draft remains clean. The per-seed statistics with proper error bars are in place, the critical alpha values have been recalculated with the right inputs, and the degeneracy-adjusted bounds are doing their job: CoNLL's bound correctly predicts limited benefit from the selection criterion, while FewNERD and SciERC's bounds align with what we're actually observing where multiview doesn't hurt performance.

The empirical results bear this out — CoNLL's 60% degeneracy explains why multiview barely moves the needle compared to greedy, which fits perfectly with the narrative that alignment, not aggregation, is the real bottleneck. I should follow up on Review #47 to confirm the correction is solid.
```
